## I In Moonfleet Village / 一 Moonfleet 村中

===Original===
“So sleeps the pride of former days.”

> Moore
===Chinese===
“旧日的荣光，就这样沉眠了。”

> Moore

===Original===
The village of Moonfleet lies half a mile from the sea on the right or west bank of the Fleet stream. This rivulet, which is so narrow as it passes the houses that I have known a good jumper clear it without a pole, broadens out into salt marshes below the village, and loses itself at last in a lake of brackish water. The lake is good for nothing except sea-fowl, herons, and oysters, and forms such a place as they call in the Indies a lagoon; being shut off from the open Channel by a monstrous great beach or dike of pebbles, of which I shall speak more hereafter. When I was a child I thought that this place was called Moonfleet, because on a still night, whether in summer, or in winter frosts, the moon shone very brightly on the lagoon; but learned afterwards that ’twas but short for “Mohune-fleet,” from the Mohunes, a great family who were once lords of all these parts.
===Chinese===
Moonfleet 村坐落在离海半英里的地方，在 Fleet 溪的右岸，也就是西岸。这条小溪流经村舍时窄得很，我见过腿脚利落的人不用撑竿就能一跃而过；到了村子下游，它便漫开成一片盐沼，最后没入一泓半咸半淡的湖水。这湖除了海鸟、苍鹭和牡蛎之外别无用处，形成了一个那种在西印度群岛被称作潟湖的地方；一道巨大无比的卵石滩——或者说是卵石堤——把它同开阔的英吉利海峡隔开，这道堤我往后还要细讲。小时候，我一直以为这地方叫做 Moonfleet，是因为无论夏天还是霜冻的冬夜，每逢无风的静夜，月亮都会明晃晃地照在这片潟湖上；后来才明白，那不过是“Mohune-fleet”的简称，出自 Mohune 家族——那曾是一个统有这一方全境的显赫大户。

===Original===
My name is John Trenchard, and I was fifteen years of age when this story begins. My father and mother had both been dead for years, and I boarded with my aunt, Miss Arnold, who was kind to me in her own fashion, but too strict and precise ever to make me love her.
===Chinese===
我叫 John Trenchard，这个故事开篇时我十五岁。父亲和母亲都已去世多年，我寄宿在姑母 Arnold 小姐家里；她按她自己的那套待我不坏，只是为人太严厉、太刻板，我始终没能爱她。

===Original===
I shall first speak of one evening in the fall of the year 1757. It must have been late in October, though I have forgotten the exact date, and I sat in the little front parlour reading after tea. My aunt had few books; a Bible, a Common Prayer, and some volumes of sermons are all that I can recollect now; but the Reverend Mr. Glennie, who taught us village children, had lent me a storybook, full of interest and adventure, called the *Arabian Nights Entertainment*. At last the light began to fail, and I was nothing loth to leave off reading for several reasons; as, first, the parlour was a chilly room with horsehair chairs and sofa, and only a coloured-paper screen in the grate, for my aunt did not allow a fire till the first of November; second, there was a rank smell of molten tallow in the house, for my aunt was dipping winter candles on frames in the back kitchen; third, I had reached a part in the *Arabian Nights* which tightened my breath and made me wish to leave off reading for very anxiousness of expectation. It was that point in the story of the “Wonderful Lamp,” where the false uncle lets fall a stone that seals the mouth of the underground chamber; and immures the boy, Aladdin, in the darkness, because he would not give up the lamp till he stood safe on the surface again. This scene reminded me of one of those dreadful nightmares, where we dream we are shut in a little room, the walls of which are closing in upon us, and so impressed me that the memory of it served as a warning in an adventure that befell me later on. So I gave up reading and stepped out into the street. It was a poor street at best, though once, no doubt, it had been finer. Now, there were not two hundred souls in Moonfleet, and yet the houses that held them straggled sadly over half a mile, lying at intervals along either side of the road. Nothing was ever made new in the village; if a house wanted repair badly, it was pulled down, and so there were toothless gaps in the street, and overrun gardens with broken-down walls, and many of the houses that yet stood looked as though they could stand but little longer.
===Chinese===
我要先讲讲 1757 年秋天的一个傍晚。那必定是十月下旬了，确切的日子我已经忘记；那天喝过茶，我坐在小小的前厅里看书。姑母的书没有几本，如今我记得起来的只有一部《圣经》、一册《公祷书》和几卷布道文集；倒是教村里孩子念书的 Glennie 牧师借给我一本故事书，妙趣横生，充满冒险，名叫《天方夜谭》。后来天色渐渐暗下来，我也巴不得搁下书不读，原因有好几桩：第一，前厅是间冷飕飕的屋子，摆着马鬃面子的椅子和沙发，壁炉里只立着一面彩纸屏风，因为不到十一月一日，姑母是不肯生火的；第二，屋子里弥漫着一股刺鼻的化牛脂气味，姑母正在后厨往烛架上蘸制过冬的蜡烛；第三，我在《天方夜谭》里正读到一个地方，攥得我气都透不过来，急切地想知道下文，只想丢开书才好。那正是《神灯》故事里的一段：假叔父抛下一块石头，封住了地下密室的洞口，把男孩阿拉丁幽禁在黑暗里，因为他不肯先交出神灯，定要等自己安全回到地面上才肯给。这一幕使我想起那种可怕的噩梦——梦见自己被关在一间小屋里，四壁正朝我们慢慢夹拢过来；它给我的印象那样深，以至后来在一场落到我头上的险遇里，这段记忆竟成了我的警示。于是我放下书，踱到街上去了。这条街即使在最好的年月也不像样，虽然从前想必也曾体面过。那时 Moonfleet 全村不足两百口人，可容纳这些人的房子却凄凄清清地拖出半英里远，零零落落地散在道路两边。村里从来不兴翻修什么；哪座房子实在破得修无可修，就索性拆掉，于是街上留下一个个豁了牙似的空档，塌了墙的园子野草丛生，许多还立着的房子看上去也支撑不了多久了。

===Original===
The sun had set; indeed, it was already so dusk that the lower or sea-end of the street was lost from sight. There was a little fog or smoke-wreath in the air, with an odour of burning weeds, and that first frosty feeling of the autumn that makes us think of glowing fires and the comfort of long winter evenings to come. All was very still, but I could hear the tapping of a hammer farther down the street, and walked to see what was doing, for we had no trades in Moonfleet save that of fishing. It was Ratsey the sexton at work in a shed which opened on the street, lettering a tombstone with a mallet and graver. He had been mason before he became fisherman, and was handy with his tools; so that if anyone wanted a headstone set up in the churchyard, he went to Ratsey to get it done. I lent over the half-door and watched him a minute, chipping away with the graver in a bad light from a lantern; then he looked up, and seeing me, said:
===Chinese===
太阳已经落山；说实在的，天色已经暗得连街道尽头靠海的那一头都望不见了。空气里浮着一缕薄雾，或者说是烟霭，带着焚烧野草的气味，还有秋天里头一场霜的那种寒意——它叫人想起红彤彤的炉火，想起将来那一个个漫长冬夜里的温暖安逸。四下里万籁俱寂，只有街那头传来叮叮的锤声，我便走过去看看在做什么营生，因为 Moonfleet 除了打鱼，再没有别的行当。原来是教堂司事 Ratsey，在一间开着门临街的棚屋里干活，正用木槌和刻刀给一块墓碑凿字。他改行打鱼之前本是石匠，使起工具来干净利落；所以谁家要在教堂墓园里立块墓碑，都去找 Ratsey 张罗。我倚在半截门上看了他一会儿，看他在灯笼昏暗的光里拿刻刀一下一下地凿；后来他抬起头来，瞧见了我，说道：

===Original===
“Here, John, if you have nothing to do, come in and hold the lantern for me, ’tis but a half-hour’s job to get all finished.”
===Chinese===
“来，John，你要是没事干，就进来替我掌着灯笼；这活儿不过半个钟头就能全部弄完。”

===Original===
Ratsey was always kind to me, and had lent me a chisel many a time to make boats, so I stepped in and held the lantern watching him chink out the bits of Portland stone with a graver, and blinking the while when they came too near my eyes. The inscription stood complete, but he was putting the finishing touches to a little sea-piece carved at the top of the stone, which showed a schooner boarding a cutter. I thought it fine work at the time, but know now that it was rough enough; indeed, you may see it for yourself in Moonfleet churchyard to this day, and read the inscription too, though it is yellow with lichen, and not so plain as it was that night. This is how it runs:
===Chinese===
Ratsey 待我一向和气，还常借刻刀给我做小船，我便跨了进去，替他掌着灯笼，看他用刻刀把波特兰石的碎屑一片片凿下来，碎屑崩得太近时，我就直眨眼睛。碑文已经凿得齐整，他正在给碑顶雕着的一小幅海景图做最后的润饰——画的是一艘纵帆船正要强登一艘单桅快船。当时我觉得这手艺真精巧，如今才知道它粗糙得很；说真的，直到今天你还可以亲自去 Moonfleet 的教堂墓园看看它，也读一读那碑文，只是它已经蒙上地衣、泛了黄，不像那天夜里那样清楚了。碑文是这样写的：

===Original===
Sacred to the memory

					of

					David Block

Aged 15, who was killed by a shot fired from the

				*Elector* Schooner, 21 June 1757.

Of life bereft (by fell design),
						

						I mingle with my fellow clay.
						

						On God’s protection I recline
						

						To save me in the Judgement Day.

There too must you, cruel man, appear,
						

						Repent ere it be all too late;
						

						Or else a dreadful sentence fear,
						

						For God will sure revenge my fate.
===Chinese===
谨此追念

					David Block

享年 15 岁，于 1757 年 6 月 21 日被“Elector”号
纵帆船一发炮弹夺去性命。

生命被夺（遭恶毒奸计），
						我与同伴的黄土同眠。
						我全然倚赖上帝的护佑，
						在审判之日救我脱险。

残忍的人啊，你也必到那里现身，
						趁一切还不算太迟，速速悔改；
						否则就惧怕那可怕的判决，
						因为上帝必为我申冤昭雪。

===Original===
The Reverend Mr. Glennie wrote the verses, and I knew them by heart, for he had given me a copy; indeed, the whole village had rung with the tale of David’s death, and it was yet in every mouth. He was only child to Elzevir Block, who kept the Why Not? inn at the bottom of the village, and was with the contrabandiers, when their ketch was boarded that June night by the government schooner. People said that it was Magistrate Maskew of Moonfleet Manor who had put the revenue men on the track, and anyway he was on board the *Elector* as she overhauled the ketch. There was some show of fighting when the vessels first came alongside of one another, and Maskew drew a pistol and fired it off in young David’s face, with only the two gunwales between them. In the afternoon of midsummer’s day the *Elector* brought the ketch into Moonfleet, and there was a posse of constables to march the smugglers off to Dorchester Jail. The prisoners trudged up through the village ironed two and two together, while people stood at their doors or followed them, the men greeting them with a kindly word, for we knew most of them as Ringstave and Monkbury men, and the women sorrowing for their wives. But they left David’s body in the ketch, so the boy paid dear for his night’s frolic.
===Chinese===
这几节诗是 Glennie 牧师写的，我早就背得烂熟，因为他给过我一份抄件；说实在的，David 死的那桩事当时传遍了全村，如今人人嘴里还念叨着。他是 Elzevir Block 的独子。Block 在村子尽头开着“Why Not?”客栈，那晚正同走私贩子们在一起，他们的双桅小帆船在六月里的那个夜晚被政府的缉私纵帆船强行登临。人们说，是 Moonfleet 庄园的 Maskew 治安官给缉私人员指点了行踪；不管怎么说，“Elector”号追上那条双桅小帆船的时候，他就在船上。两船刚一靠帮，多少摆出了些厮杀的架势，Maskew 拔出一支手枪，隔着两道船舷，冲着少年 David 的脸放了一枪。仲夏节那天下午，“Elector”号把双桅小帆船押进了 Moonfleet，有一队差役正等着把走私犯押往多切斯特监狱。囚犯们两人一组锁着铁镣，一路蹚过村子；人们站在自家门口，或者跟在后面，男人们同他们亲切地道别，因为这些人大多数是 Ringstave 和 Monkbury 的，我们都相熟；女人们则为他们的妻子伤心落泪。可是 David 的尸体被撂在了双桅小帆船上——这孩子为一夜的荒唐，付出了太惨痛的代价。

===Original===
“Ay, ’twas a cruel, cruel thing to fire on so young a lad,” Ratsey said, as he stepped back a pace to study the effect of a flag that he was chiselling on the revenue schooner, “and trouble is likely to come to the other poor fellows taken, for Lawyer Empson says three of them will surely hang at next assize. I recollect,” he went on, “thirty years ago, when there was a bit of a scuffle between the *Royal Sophy* and the *Marnhull*, they hanged four of the contrabandiers, and my old father caught his death of cold what with going to see the poor chaps turned off at Dorchester, and standing up to his knees in the river Frome to get a sight of them, for all the countryside was there, and such a press there was no place on land. There, that’s enough,” he said, turning again to the gravestone. “On Monday I’ll line the ports in black, and get a brush of red to pick out the flag; and now, my son, you’ve helped with the lantern, so come down to the Why Not? and there I’ll have a word with Elzevir, who sadly needs the talk of kindly friends to cheer him, and we’ll find you a glass of Hollands to keep out autumn chills.”
===Chinese===
“是啊，朝这么年轻的后生开枪，实在是狠毒、狠毒的事，”Ratsey 说，一边退后一步，端详他在那艘缉私船上凿着的旗子的效果，“被抓的另外几个可怜人多半也要遭殃，因为 Empson 律师说，其中三个下季巡回法庭开审时准得绞死。我记得，”他接着说，“三十年前，‘Royal Sophy’号和‘Marnhull’号闹过一场小小的冲突，他们绞死了四个走私贩子；我那老父亲赶去多切斯特看那几个可怜人上路，为了瞧上一眼，站在弗罗姆河里水没到膝盖的地方，结果染上风寒，送了性命——因为四乡八里的人都到了，岸上人挤人，连个站脚的地方都没有。好了，不说了，”他说着又转回墓碑跟前，“星期一我要把炮门描成黑的，再找一支红笔把旗子衬出来。现在，孩子，你帮我掌了灯笼，就跟我一起到‘Why Not?’去吧，我要跟 Elzevir 说句话，他太需要好心朋友说说话、宽宽心了；我们还要给你找一杯荷兰杜松子酒，好挡挡秋天的寒气。”

===Original===
I was but a lad, and thought it a vast honour to be asked to the Why Not?﻿—for did not such an invitation raise me at once to the dignity of manhood? Ah, sweet boyhood, how eager are we as boys to be quit of thee, with what regret do we look back on thee before our man’s race is halfway run! Yet was not my pleasure without alloy, for I feared even to think of what Aunt Jane would say if she knew that I had been at the Why Not?﻿—and beside that, I stood in awe of grim old Elzevir Block, grimmer and sadder a thousand times since David’s death.
===Chinese===
我那时不过是个孩子，觉得人家请我上“Why Not?”去，是天大的体面——这样的邀约，岂不是一下子把我抬举成了堂堂的男子汉？啊，甜蜜的少年时光！我们做孩子的时候，多么急切地想挣脱你；可成年人的路程还没跑上一半，回头望你，又是多么怅惘！不过我的欢喜也不是纯净无杂的：光是一想到 Jane 姑妈若是知道我上过“Why Not?”会说出什么话来，我就心惊；何况，我还惧怕阴沉的老 Elzevir Block——自 David 死后，他比先前更阴沉、更悲苦了一千倍。

===Original===
The Why Not? was not the real name of the inn; it was properly the Mohune Arms. The Mohunes had once owned, as I have said, the whole of the village; but their fortunes fell, and with them fell the fortunes of Moonfleet. The ruins of their mansion showed grey on the hillside above the village; their almshouses stood halfway down the street, with the quadrangle deserted and overgrown; the Mohune image and superscription was on everything from the church to the inn, and everything that bore it was stamped also with the superscription of decay. And here it is necessary that I say a few words as to this family badge; for, as you will see, I was to bear it all my life, and shall carry its impress with me to the grave. The Mohune shield was plain white or silver, and bore nothing upon it except a great black “Y.” I call it a “Y,” though the Reverend Mr. Glennie once explained to me that it was not a “Y” at all, but what heralds call a *cross-pall.* *Cross-pall* or no *cross-pall*, it looked for all the world like a black “Y,” with a broad arm ending in each of the top corners of the shield, and the tail coming down into the bottom. You might see that cognizance carved on the manor, and on the stonework and woodwork of the church, and on a score of houses in the village, and it hung on the signboard over the door of the inn. Everyone knew the Mohune “Y” for miles around, and a former landlord having called the inn the Why Not? in jest, the name had stuck to it ever since.
===Chinese===
“Why Not?”并不是这家客栈的本名；它正儿八经的字号是 Mohune Arms（Mohune 家徽）。正如我说过的，Mohune 家族曾经拥有整座村庄；后来他们家道中落，Moonfleet 的气数也随着败落了。他们宅邸的废墟在村子上方的山坡上泛着灰色；他们的济贫院立在街道中段，四方院子荒废无人、草木丛生；从教堂到客栈，样样东西上都有 Mohune 家的徽像和题铭，而凡是带着这徽记的东西，也一概烙上了衰败的印记。关于这家族的徽记，这里我必须赘言几句；因为诸位往后会看到，我这一生都要佩戴着它，直到进了坟墓，还要带着它的印痕。Mohune 家的盾徽是素白的，或者说银白的底，上面什么花样也没有，只有一个大大的黑色“Y”字。我管它叫“Y”，虽然 Glennie 牧师有一回对我解释过，说它压根儿不是什么“Y”，而是纹章学家所谓的 *cross-pall*。管它是不是 *cross-pall*，它看上去活脱脱就是个黑色的“Y”字：两条宽臂分别伸到盾面上方的两角，一条尾巴垂进盾底。在庄园宅邸上，在教堂的石作和木作上，在村里几十座房子上，你都能看到这个徽记；它还悬在客栈门楣的招牌上。方圆几英里内，人人都认得 Mohune 家的“Y”字；从前有位店主打着趣，给客栈起了“Why Not?”（何不呢？）这个名字，从此这名字就再也没能甩掉。

===Original===
More than once on winter evenings, when men were drinking in the Why Not?, I had stood outside, and listened to them singing “Ducky-stones,” or “Kegs bobbing One, Two, Three,” or some of the other tunes that sailors sing in the west. Such songs had neither beginning nor ending, and very little sense to catch hold of in the middle. One man would crone the air, and the others would crone a solemn chorus, but there was little hard drinking, for Elzevir Block never got drunk himself, and did not like his guests to get drunk either. On singing nights the room grew hot, and the steam stood so thick on the glass inside that one could not see in; but at other times, when there was no company, I have peeped through the red curtains and watched Elzevir Block and Ratsey playing backgammon at the trestle-table by the fire. It was on the trestle-table that Block had afterwards laid out his son’s dead body, and some said they had looked through the window at night and seen the father trying to wash the blood-matting out of the boy’s yellow hair, and heard him groaning and talking to the lifeless clay as if it could understand. Anyhow, there had been little drinking in the inn since that time, for Block grew more and more silent and morose. He had never courted customers, and now he scowled on any that came, so that men looked on the Why Not? as a blighted spot, and went to drink at the Three Choughs at Ringstave.
===Chinese===
不止一个冬天的夜晚，男人们聚在“Why Not?”里喝酒，我曾站在外面，听他们唱《鸭子石》，唱《酒桶颠啊一、二、三》，或是西部水手们传唱的别的什么小调。这类歌既没有开头，也没有结尾，中间也抓不着多少意思。一个人哼起调子，其余的人便低声哼起庄严的副歌；不过没有什么人喝得烂醉，因为 Elzevir Block 自己从不喝醉，也不喜欢客人醉倒。唱歌的夜晚，屋里变得燥热，窗玻璃内侧凝着厚厚一层水汽，外头什么也望不见；可在别的没有客人的时候，我从红窗帘缝里偷看过 Elzevir Block 和 Ratsey 坐在炉边那张搁板桌旁下双陆棋。后来，Block 就是在那张搁板桌上安放他儿子尸身的；有人说，他们夜里从窗外望见这位父亲想把结成血痂的污垢从孩子的黄头发里洗出来，听见他呻吟着，对那没有生命的躯壳说话，仿佛它还听得懂似的。总之，打那以后，店里就很少有人喝酒了，因为 Block 越来越沉默，越来越乖僻。他从来不去招揽客人，如今谁来他冲谁皱眉瞪眼，于是人们把“Why Not?”看作一处遭了灾的地方，宁可上 Ringstave 的“三寒鸦”去喝。

===Original===
My heart was in my mouth when Ratsey lifted the latch and led me into the inn parlour. It was a low sanded room with no light except a fire of seawood on the hearth, burning clear and lambent with blue salt flames. There were tables at each end of the room, and wooden-seated chairs round the walls, and at the trestle table by the chimney sat Elzevir Block smoking a long pipe and looking at the fire. He was a man of fifty, with a shock of grizzled hair, a broad but not unkindly face of regular features, bushy eyebrows, and the finest forehead that I ever saw. His frame was thickset, and still immensely strong; indeed, the countryside was full of tales of his strange prowess or endurance. Blocks had been landlords at the Why Not? father and son for years, but Elzevir’s mother came from the Low Countries, and that was how he got his outland name and could speak Dutch. Few men knew much of him, and folks often wondered how it was he kept the Why Not? on so little custom as went that way. Yet he never seemed to lack for money; and if people loved to tell stories of his strength, they would speak also of widows helped, and sick comforted with unknown gifts, and hint that some of them came from Elzevir Block for all he was so grim and silent.
===Chinese===
Ratsey 拉开门闩，领我进了客栈的店堂，我的心都提到了嗓子眼。那是一间低矮的、地上铺着细沙的屋子，没有点灯，只有壁炉里一堆浮木燃着的火，烧得澄澈透亮，跳动着盐分的蓝色火苗。屋子两头各有一张桌子，沿墙四下摆着木面坐椅；烟囱旁那张搁板桌边，坐着 Elzevir Block，抽着一支长烟斗，望着炉火。他五十岁上下，一头浓密的灰白乱发，一张宽阔却不失慈和的端正脸膛，两道浓眉，还有我平生所见最饱满的额头。他身量敦实，力气依旧大得吓人；说真的，乡间到处流传着他神力和耐性的奇闻。Block 家在“Why Not?”父传子、子传孙，当了许多年店主；不过 Elzevir 的母亲是低地国家的人，他那个外国名字便是这么来的，还会讲荷兰话。很少有人深知他的底细，乡亲们常常纳闷，凭着那点寥寥的生意，他怎么把“Why Not?”支撑下来。可他从来不曾短过钱使；人们固然爱讲他力气的种种传闻，也会说起受接济的寡妇、得到无名馈赠而宽慰的病人，还暗示其中有几桩就出自 Elzevir Block——别看他那么阴沉寡言。

===Original===
He turned round and got up as we came in, and my fears led me to think that his face darkened when he saw me.
===Chinese===
我们进屋时，他转过身子站了起来；我心怀畏惧，只觉得他一看见我，脸色就沉了下来。

===Original===
“What does this boy want?” he said to Ratsey sharply.
===Chinese===
“这孩子来干什么？”他没好气地对 Ratsey 说。

===Original===
“He wants the same as I want, and that’s a glass of Ararat milk to keep out autumn chills,” the sexton answered, drawing another chair up to the trestle-table.
===Chinese===
“他想要的跟我想的一样，就是一杯亚拉腊乳，好挡挡秋寒，”司事答道，一面又拖了张椅子到搁板桌旁。

===Original===
“Cows’ milk is best for children such as he,” was Elzevir’s answer, as he took two shining brass candlesticks from the mantel-board, set them on the table, and lit the candles with a burning chip from the hearth.
===Chinese===
“像他这样的孩子，喝牛奶最合适。”Elzevir 答道。他从壁炉台上取下两只锃亮的黄铜烛台，摆到桌上，又从炉膛里夹起一块烧着的木片，点亮了蜡烛。

===Original===
“John is no child; he is the same age as David, and comes from helping me to finish David’s headstone. ’Tis finished now, barring the paint upon the ships, and, please God, by Monday night we will have it set fair and square in the churchyard, and then the poor lad may rest in peace, knowing he has above him Master Ratsey’s best handiwork, and the parson’s verses to set forth how shamefully he came to his end.”
===Chinese===
“John 才不是孩子了；他跟 David 同岁，刚才是帮我做完 David 的墓碑来的。如今碑都刻成了，只差给船描漆。托上帝的福，到星期一夜里，我们就能把它端端正正地立进教堂墓园，那时这可怜的孩子就可以安息了——知道头顶上有 Ratsey 师傅最出色的手艺，还有牧师的诗句，替他昭告他是怎样含冤惨死的。”

===Original===
I thought that Elzevir softened a little as Ratsey spoke of his son, and he said, “Ay, David rests in peace. ’Tis they that brought him to his end that shall not rest in peace when their time comes. And it may come sooner than they think,” he added, speaking more to himself than to us. I knew that he meant Mr. Maskew, and recollected that some had warned the magistrate that he had better keep out of Elzevir’s way, for there was no knowing what a desperate man might do. And yet the two had met since in the village street, and nothing worse come of it than a scowling look from Block.
===Chinese===
Ratsey 提起他儿子的时候，我觉得 Elzevir 的神色柔和了一些。他说：“是啊，David 是安息了。可那些把他送上绝路的人，等他们的时辰一到，就休想安息。而这时辰，说不定比他们想的来得还早。”他又添上一句，与其说是讲给我们听，不如说是自言自语。我知道他指的是 Maskew 先生，也想起从前有人劝过这位治安官，最好躲着 Elzevir 走，因为一个横下心的亡命汉，什么事都干得出来。然而打那以后，两人也在村街上照过面，除 Block 恶狠狠瞪了一眼之外，并没有出更糟的事。

===Original===
“Tush, man!” broke in the sexton, “it was the foulest deed ever man did; but let not thy mind brood on it, nor think how thou mayest get thyself avenged. Leave that to Providence; for He whose wisdom lets such things be done, will surely see they meet their due reward. ‘Vengeance is Mine; I will repay, saith the Lord.’ ” And he took his hat off and hung it on a peg.
===Chinese===
“嗐，老哥！”司事插嘴道，“这是人干得出的最下作的勾当；可你别让心里老盘算着它，也别去想你怎么替自己报仇。把那交给天意吧；上帝的智慧既容许这样的事做成，就必定看着他们受到应得的报应。‘伸冤在我，我必报应，这是主说的。’”说着，他摘下帽子，挂到衣钩上。

===Original===
Block did not answer, but set three glasses on the table, and then took out from a cupboard a little round long-necked bottle, from which he poured out a glass for Ratsey and himself. Then he half-filled the third, and pushed it along the table to me, saying, “There, take it, lad, if thou wilt; ’twill do thee no good, but may do thee no harm.”
===Chinese===
Block 没有答话，只在桌上摆了三只杯子，然后从碗柜里取出一只圆滚滚的长颈小瓶，给自己和 Ratsey 各斟了一杯。接着他把第三只杯子斟到半满，顺着桌面推到我面前，说：“拿去吧，孩子，你要是愿意就喝；这东西对你没什么好处，可也没什么害处。”

===Original===
Ratsey raised his glass almost before it was filled. He sniffed the liquor and smacked his lips. “O rare milk of Ararat!” he said, “it is sweet and strong, and sets the heart at ease. And now get the backgammon-board, John, and set it for us on the table.” So they fell to the game, and I took a sly sip at the liquor, but nearly choked myself, not being used to strong waters, and finding it heady and burning in the throat. Neither man spoke, and there was no sound except the constant rattle of the dice, and the rubbing of the pieces being moved across the board. Now and then one of the players stopped to light his pipe, and at the end of a game they scored their totals on the table with a bit of chalk. So I watched them for an hour, knowing the game myself, and being interested at seeing Elzevir’s backgammon-board, which I had heard talked of before.
===Chinese===
Ratsey 的酒还没斟满就举起了杯子。他嗅了嗅酒，咂了咂嘴。“啊，稀世的亚拉腊乳！”他说，“又甜又烈，喝下去心里踏实。好了 John，去把双陆棋盘拿来，给我们摆到桌上。”于是两人下起棋来；我偷偷抿了一小口那酒，却险些呛死——我不惯喝烈性的酒，只觉得它冲头，烧喉咙。两个人都不说话，除了骰子不停的嗒嗒声和棋子贴着棋盘推来推去的摩擦声，屋里再没有一点响动。下棋的人不时停下来点烟斗，一局终了，两人便用一小截粉笔把总数记在桌上。我就这样看了他们一个钟头——这棋我自己也会下，而且早就听人说起过 Elzevir 那副双陆棋盘，如今见了，自然格外留心。

===Original===
It had formed part of the furniture of the Why Not? for generations of landlords, and served perhaps to pass time for cavaliers of the Civil Wars. All was of oak, black and polished, board, dice-boxes, and men, but round the edge ran a Latin inscription inlaid in light wood, which I read on that first evening, but did not understand till Mr. Glennie translated it to me. I had cause to remember it afterwards, so I shall set it down here in Latin for those who know that tongue, *ita in vita ut in lusu alae pessima jactura arte corrigenda est*, and in English as Mr. Glennie translated it, “As in life, so in a game of hazard, skill will make something of the worst of throws.” At last Elzevir looked up and spoke to me, not unkindly, “Lad, it is time for you to go home; men say that Blackbeard walks on the first nights of winter, and some have met him face to face betwixt this house and yours.” I saw he wanted to be rid of me, so bade them both good night, and was off home, running all the way thither, though not from any fear of Blackbeard, for Ratsey had often told me that there was no chance of meeting him unless one passed the churchyard by night.
===Chinese===
这棋盘在“Why Not?”里经历了一代又一代店主，算得上店里的老家什了，英国内战年间的保王党骑士们，说不定还靠它消磨过时光。棋盘、骰子筒和棋子全是橡木做的，乌黑发亮；只有四边用浅色木头嵌着一圈拉丁文铭文——那头一晚我读了一遍，直到 Glennie 先生给我讲解，才明白它的意思。后来我有充分的理由记住这句话，所以在这里先用拉丁文把它录下来，给懂得那种语言的人看：*ita in vita ut in lusu alae pessima jactura arte corrigenda est*；再照 Glennie 先生的英译录出：“人生如同赌戏，纵使掷出最坏的骰运，技艺也能有所补救。”最后，Elzevir 抬起头来对我说话，语气并不严厉：“孩子，你该回家了；人们说，入冬头几个夜里 Blackbeard（黑胡子）会出来走动，有人就在这屋子和你家之间，同他打了个照面。”我看得出他是想打发我走，便向两人道了晚安，一路跑回了家——倒不是怕 Blackbeard，因为 Ratsey 常对我说，若不是夜里打教堂墓园经过，是决计碰不上他的。

===Original===
Blackbeard was one of the Mohunes who had died a century back, and was buried in the vault under the church, with others of his family, but could not rest there, whether, as some said, because he was always looking for a lost treasure, or as others, because of his exceeding wickedness in life. If this last were the true reason, he must have been bad indeed, for Mohunes have died before and since his day wicked enough to bear anyone company in their vault or elsewhere. Men would have it that on dark winter nights Blackbeard might be seen with an old-fashioned lantern digging for treasure in the graveyard; and those who professed to know said he was the tallest of men, with full black beard, coppery face, and such evil eyes, that any who once met their gaze must die within a year. However that might be, there were few in Moonfleet who would not rather walk ten miles round than go near the churchyard after dark; and once when Cracky Jones, a poor doited body, was found there one summer morning, lying dead on the grass, it was thought that he had met Blackbeard in the night.
===Chinese===
Blackbeard 是 Mohune 家族里的一个人，已经死了一百年了，葬在教堂底下的墓窖里，同他的族人做伴；可在那儿也安息不了——有人说，是因为他总在寻一件失落的财宝；也有人说，是因为他生前作恶太甚。若这后一种说法是真的，那他一定坏透了，因为在他之前和之后，Mohune 家都有人坏得足够在墓窖里或随便什么地方给任何人作伴。人们言之凿凿，说昏黑的冬夜里，可以看见 Blackbeard 提着一盏老式灯笼在墓园里掘宝；那些自称知情的人还说，他个子高得出奇，蓄着一部浓密的黑胡子，一张紫铜色的脸，一双眼睛邪气逼人，谁要是同那目光对上一眼，不出一年准死。不管真相怎样，Moonfleet 很少有人肯在天黑之后走近教堂墓园，宁肯多绕上十英里路；有一回，一个叫 Cracky Jones 的疯疯傻傻的可怜人，夏日的一个清晨被发现直挺挺死在墓园的草地上，大家都说，他夜里是撞见 Blackbeard 了。

===Original===
Mr. Glennie, who knew more about such things than anyone else, told me that Blackbeard was none other than a certain Colonel John Mohune, deceased about one hundred years ago. He would have it that Colonel Mohune, in the dreadful wars against King Charles the First, had deserted the allegiance of his house and supported the cause of the rebels. So being made Governor of Carisbrooke Castle for the parliament, he became there the king’s jailer, but was false to his trust. For the king, carrying constantly hidden about his person a great diamond which had once been given him by his brother King of France, Mohune got wind of this jewel, and promised that if it were given him he would wink at His Majesty’s escape. Then this wicked man, having taken the bribe, plays traitor again, comes with a file of soldiers at the hour appointed for the king’s flight, finds His Majesty escaping through a window, has him away to a stricter ward, and reports to the parliament that the king’s escape is only prevented by Colonel Mohune’s watchfulness. But how true, as Mr. Glennie said, that we should not be envious against the ungodly, against the man that walketh after evil counsels. Suspicion fell on Colonel Mohune; he was removed from his governorship, and came back to his home at Moonfleet. There he lived in seclusion, despised by both parties in the State, until he died, about the time of the happy restoration of King Charles the Second. But even after his death he could not get rest; for men said that he had hid somewhere that treasure given him to permit the king’s escape, and that not daring to reclaim it, had let the secret die with him, and so must needs come out of his grave to try to get at it again. Mr. Glennie would never say whether he believed the tale or not, pointing out that apparitions both of good and evil spirits are related in Holy Scripture, but that the churchyard was an unlikely spot for Colonel Mohune to seek his treasure in; for had it been buried there, he would have had a hundred chances to have it up in his lifetime. However this may be, though I was brave as a lion by day, and used indeed to frequent the churchyard, because there was the widest view of the sea to be obtained from it, yet no reward would have taken me thither at night. Nor was I myself without some witness to the tale, for having to walk to Ringstave for Dr. Hawkins on the night my aunt broke her leg, I took the path along the down which overlooks the churchyard at a mile off; and thence most certainly saw a light moving to and fro about the church, where no honest man could be at two o’clock in the morning.
===Chinese===
Glennie 先生对这类事情比谁都见多识广。他告诉我，Blackbeard 不是别人，正是 John Mohune 上校，大约一百年前去世。照他的说法，Mohune 上校在那场对抗查理一世的惨烈内战里，背弃了家族世代效忠的立场，去附从了叛党。他因此被国会任命为卡里斯布鲁克城堡的总督，在那里当上了国王的狱卒，却辜负了这份信托。原来，国王身上常年贴身藏着一颗大钻石，那是他的王兄法兰西国王从前赠给他的；Mohune 打听到了这颗宝石的风声，便许诺说，只要宝石归他，国王陛下越狱的事他就睁一只眼闭一只眼。于是这个恶人受了贿，再次背信弃义：在约定国王出逃的钟点，带着一小队士兵赶到，撞见陛下正从窗口往外逃，便把他押到看守更严的牢房里去，还向国会呈报，说全亏 Mohune 上校警惕，国王的出逃才未能得逞。可是 Glennie 先生说得多对啊：我们不该因恶人心怀不平，不该嫉妒那行恶计的人。嫌疑落到了 Mohune 上校头上；他被撤了总督之职，回到 Moonfleet 的家中。此后他深居简出，为国家两党所不齿，直到死去——那大约正是查理二世欣然复辟的时节。可就连死了，他也得不到安息；人们说，他把那笔受贿放走国王的财宝藏在了什么地方，又不敢去起出来，只得让秘密随他一同埋进坟墓，于是他不得不从坟墓里出来，想再把财宝弄到手。Glennie 先生从来不肯说他信不信这个故事，只指出善灵与恶灵显现的事，《圣经》里都有记载，可教堂墓园不大像是 Mohune 上校寻宝的地方；因为财宝若真埋在那里，他生前有的是机会，早该起出来一百回了。且不管这些，我白天胆大如狮，也确实常去教堂墓园，因为从那儿望海，眼界最开阔；可一到夜里，给多大的赏钱也别想让我去。而且我自己对这传说也并非没有见证：姑母摔断腿的那天夜里，我步行去 Ringstave 请 Hawkins 医生，走的是山岗上那条小路，从那里可以俯瞰一英里外的教堂墓园；就在那儿，我千真万确地看见一点火光在教堂附近移来移去——凌晨两点钟，正经人是决不会在那儿的。

## Epigraph / 题词

===Original===
We thought there was no more behind
					

					But such a day tomorrow as today
					

					And to be a boy eternal.

> Shakespeare
===Chinese===
我们原以为身后别无其他，

只盼明日也如今日一般，

而少年永驻，长为少年。

> 莎士比亚

## II The Floods / 二 洪水

===Original===
“Then banks came down with ruin and rout,
						

						Then beaten spray flew round about,
						

						Then all the mighty floods were out,
						

						And all the world was in the sea.”

> Jean Ingelow
===Chinese===
“于是堤岸崩摧，溃乱倾覆，
						于是激碎的浪花四下飞洒，
						于是滔滔洪水尽数泛滥，
						整个世界尽没于汪洋大海。”

> Jean Ingelow

===Original===
On the third of November, a few days after this visit to the Why Not?, the wind, which had been blowing from the southwest, began about four in the afternoon to rise in sudden strong gusts. The rooks had been pitch-falling all the morning, so we knew that bad weather was due; and when we came out from the schooling that Mr. Glennie gave us in the hall of the old almshouses, there were wisps of thatch, and even stray tiles, flying from the roofs, and the children sang:
===Chinese===
十一月三日，也就是上“Why Not?”去过没几天，原本一直从西南方向吹来的风，下午四点钟光景，忽然一阵紧似一阵地猛刮起来。整个上午，白嘴鸦都在半空里栽着跟头往下坠，所以我们知道坏天气快要来了；我们从 Glennie 先生教课的老济贫院大厅里出来时，只见一绺绺的茅草，连零星的碎瓦，都从屋顶上飞了起来，孩子们唱道：

===Original===
“Blow wind, rise storm,
					

					Ship ashore before morn.”
===Chinese===
“风儿刮，暴风起，
						船儿天亮前上岸去。”

===Original===
It is heathenish rhyme that has come down out of other and worse times; for though I do not say but that a wreck on Moonfleet beach was looked upon sometimes as little short of a godsend, yet I hope none of us were so wicked as to *wish* a vessel to be wrecked that we might share in the plunder. Indeed, I have known the men of Moonfleet risk their own lives a hundred times to save those of shipwrecked mariners, as when the *Darius*, East Indiaman, came ashore; nay, even poor nameless corpses washed up were sure of Christian burial, or perhaps of one of Master Ratsey’s headstones to set forth sex and date, as may be seen in the churchyard to this day.
===Chinese===
这是从别的、更糟的年月里传下来的一首异教歪诗；因为虽说我不敢否认，Moonfleet 海滩上出的海难，有时候简直被大家看作老天爷的赏赐，但我盼望我们中间还没有谁坏到存心*盼着*一条船失事，好去分一杯赃物。说真的，我见过 Moonfleet 的人上百次豁出性命，去救失事船上的水手，就像东印度商船“Darius”号搁浅那回一样；不但如此，就连冲上岸来的无名浮尸，也必定得到基督教的安葬，或许还能得一块 Ratsey 师傅立的墓碑，写明性别和日期——这情形直到今天还看得见，就在教堂墓园里。

===Original===
Our village lies near the centre of Moonfleet Bay, a great bight twenty miles across, and a deathtrap to up-channel sailors in a southwesterly gale. For with that wind blowing strong from south, if you cannot double the snout, you must most surely come ashore; and many a good ship failing to round that point has beat up and down the bay all day, but come to beach in the evening. And once on the beach, the sea has little mercy, for the water is deep right in, and the waves curl over full on the pebbles with a weight no timbers can withstand. Then if poor fellows try to save themselves, there is a deadly undertow or rush back of the water, which sucks them off their legs, and carries them again under the thundering waves. It is that back-suck of the pebbles that you may hear for miles inland, even at Dorchester, on still nights long after the winds that caused it have sunk, and which makes people turn in their beds, and thank God they are not fighting with the sea on Moonfleet beach.
===Chinese===
我们村坐落在 Moonfleet 湾将近正中的地方。那是个宽达二十英里的大海湾，对顶着西南大风在海峡里赶路的水手，就是个催命的陷阱。因为南边那样的大风紧刮着，你若绕不过那道海岬，就十拿九稳得上岸；多少好船没能绕过那个岬角，在湾里抢风折腾了一整天，到傍晚还是搁上了海滩。而一旦上了海滩，大海是没有什么怜悯的：岸边的海水直上直下地深，海浪整个儿卷起来，结结实实砸在卵石上，那分量是任何船骨也吃不消的。这时候可怜人若想自救，还有一股要命的回头浪——海水凶猛地倒卷回来，把他们从脚上吸倒，又裹进雷鸣般的浪头底下去。卵石上那阵倒吸的轰响，能往内陆传出好几英里，连多切斯特都听得见；在酿成它的风早已平息之后很久，静夜里还响着，叫人在床上翻来覆去，感谢上帝：自己此刻不是在 Moonfleet 海滩上同大海拼命。

===Original===
But on this third of November there was no wreck, only such a wind as I have never known before, and only once since. All night long the tempest grew fiercer, and I think no one in Moonfleet went to bed; for there was such a breaking of tiles and glass, such a banging of doors and rattling of shutters, that no sleep was possible, and we were afraid besides lest the chimneys should fall and crush us. The wind blew fiercest about five in the morning, and then some ran up the street calling out a new danger﻿—that the sea was breaking over the beach, and that all the place was like to be flooded. Some of the women were for flitting forthwith and climbing the down; but Master Ratsey, who was going round with others to comfort people, soon showed us that the upper part of the village stood so high, that if the water was to get thither, there was no knowing if it would not cover Ridgedown itself. But what with its being a spring-tide, and the sea breaking clean over the great outer beach of pebbles﻿—a thing that had not happened for fifty years﻿—there was so much water piled up in the lagoon, that it passed its bounds and flooded all the sea meadows, and even the lower end of the street. So when day broke, there was the churchyard flooded, though ’twas on rising ground, and the church itself standing up like a steep little island, and the water over the doorsill of the Why Not?, though Elzevir Block would not budge, saying he did not care if the sea swept him away. It was but a nine-hours’ wonder, for the wind fell very suddenly; the water began to go back, the sun shone bright, and before noon people came out to the doors to see the floods and talk over the storm. Most said that never had been so fierce a wind, but some of the oldest spoke of one in the second year of Queen Anne, and would have it as bad or worse. But whether worse or not, this storm was a weighty matter enough for me, and turned the course of my life, as you shall hear.
===Chinese===
可是这个十一月三日，并没有出什么海难，只是刮了一场我此前从未领教过、后来也只再领教过一回的大风。一整夜暴风越刮越猛，我想 Moonfleet 没有一个人上过床；瓦片和玻璃噼里啪啦地碎，门扇砰砰地撞，百叶窗哐啷哐啷地响，谁也别想睡着；何况我们还提心吊胆，生怕烟囱倒下来把我们砸扁。风刮得最凶是在清晨五点钟前后，这时有人沿街跑上来喊新的险情——说海浪正整个儿漫过海滩，这地方眼看就要全淹了。有些女人主张马上搬家，爬上山岗去躲；可是正挨家挨户安慰大家的 Ratsey 师傅很快就让我们明白：村子高处站得那么高，水若是都能淹到那里，天晓得会不会连 Ridgedown 本身都盖过去。无奈那天正赶上大潮，海水整个儿漫过了外面那道巨大的卵石堤——这是五十年没有过的事——潟湖里积起那么多水，漫出了界，淹掉了全部海边草甸，连街道靠下的那一头也淹了。于是天一亮，教堂墓园进了水——虽然它在高地上；教堂孤零零立着，像一座陡峭的小岛；水漫过了“Why Not?”的门槛，尽管 Elzevir Block 说什么也不肯挪窝，说哪怕大海把他卷了去，他也满不在乎。这不过是一桩热闹了九个钟头的新鲜事，因为风说停就停；水开始往回退，太阳明晃晃地照着，不到中午，人们已经走到门口看洪水、议论这场风暴了。大多数人说不曾有过这么凶的风，可几位最年长的老人说起安妮女王二年那一场，一口咬定不相上下，或者更凶。但不管凶不凶，这场风暴对我说来已是件天大的事，它扭转了我一生的道路——诸位往下听就知道了。

===Original===
I have said that the waters came up so high that the church stood out like an island; but they went back quickly, and Mr. Glennie was able to hold service on the next Sunday morning. Few enough folks came to Moonfleet Church at any time; but fewer still came that morning, for the meadows between the village and the churchyard were wet and miry from the water. There were streamers of seaweed tangled about the very tombstones, and against the outside of the churchyard wall was piled up a great bank of it, from which came a salt rancid smell like a guillemot’s egg that is always in the air after a southwesterly gale has strewn the shore with wrack.
===Chinese===
我已经说过，水涨得那么高，教堂像座岛似的立在水中央；不过水退得快，到下一个礼拜天早晨，Glennie 先生照常主持了礼拜。Moonfleet 教堂平日来的人本就不多；那天早晨来得更少，因为村子同教堂墓园之间的草甸叫水泡得又湿又烂。一绺绺的海草缠在墓碑上，教堂墓园外墙根还堆着一大垛，散出一股咸腥的哈喇味，活像海鸠蛋的气味——每逢西南大风把海草撒满海岸，空气里总少不了这一股味儿。

===Original===
This church is as large as any other I have seen, and divided into two parts with a stone screen across the middle. Perhaps Moonfleet was once a large place, and then likely enough there were people to fill such a church, but never since I knew it did anyone worship in that part called the nave. This western portion was quite empty beyond a few old tombs and a Royal Arms of Queen Anne; the pavement too was damp and mossy; and there were green patches down the white walls where the rains had got in. So the handful of people that came to church were glad enough to get the other side of the screen in the chancel, where at least the pew floors were boarded over, and the panelling of oak-work kept off the draughts.
===Chinese===
这座教堂同我见过的任何一座一样大，中间横着一道石屏，分作两半。或许 Moonfleet 从前是个大地方，那时候很可能真有人把这样一座教堂坐满；可从我记事以来，从没有人到叫作中殿的那一半去做过礼拜。靠西的这半边空空荡荡，只有几座古墓和一幅安妮女王在位时的王室纹徽；石板地面潮乎乎地生了苔，白墙上雨水渗漏过的地方淌下一条条绿斑。所以来做礼拜的那一小撮人都乐于坐到石屏那一边的圣坛里去，那儿至少座位底下铺了木板，橡木护板也挡得住穿堂风。

===Original===
Now this Sunday morning there were only three or four, I think, beside Mr. Glennie and Ratsey and the half-dozen of us boys, who crossed the swampy meadows strewn with drowned shrewmice and moles. Even my aunt was not at church, being prevented by a migraine, but a surprise waited those who did go, for there in a pew by himself sat Elzevir Block. The people stared at him as they came in, for no one had ever known him go to church before; some saying in the village that he was a Catholic, and others an infidel. However that may be, there he was this day, wishing perhaps to show a favour to the parson who had written the verses for David’s headstone. He took no notice of anyone, nor exchanged greetings with those that came in, as was the fashion in Moonfleet Church, but kept his eyes fixed on a prayerbook which he held in his hand, though he could not be following the minister, for he never turned the leaf.
===Chinese===
这个礼拜天的早晨，我想，除了 Glennie 先生、Ratsey，和我们六个跨过泥泞草甸——上面撒满了淹死的鼩鼱和鼹鼠——的男孩，来的也就三四个人了。连我的姑母都没来做礼拜，叫偏头痛绊住了。可是来的人却迎面撞上一桩意外：Elzevir Block 独自一人坐在一处座位上。人们进门时都直着眼瞧他，因为从没有人见他进过教堂；村里有人说他是天主教徒，也有人说他是个不信神的人。不管怎么说，这一天他坐在那里，也许是想对那位替 David 的墓碑写过诗句的牧师表示一点好意。他不理会任何人，也不照 Moonfleet 教堂的规矩，同进来的人互道平安，只把眼睛盯在手里的一册《公祷书》上，尽管他并没有跟着牧师念——因为他一页也没有翻过。

===Original===
The church was so damp from the floods, that Master Ratsey had put a fire in the brazier which stood at the back, but was not commonly lighted till the winter had fairly begun. We boys sat as close to the brazier as we could, for the wet cold struck up from the flags, and besides that, we were so far from the clergyman, and so well screened by the oak backs, that we could bake an apple or roast a chestnut without much fear of being caught. But that morning there was something else to take off our thoughts; for before the service was well begun, we became aware of a strange noise under the church. The first time it came was just as Mr. Glennie was finishing “Dearly Beloved,” and we heard it again before the second lesson. It was not a loud noise, but rather like that which a boat makes jostling against another at sea, only there was something deeper and more hollow about it. We boys looked at each other, for we knew what was under the church, and that the sound could only come from the Mohune vault. No one at Moonfleet had ever seen the inside of that vault; but Ratsey was told by his father, who was clerk before him, that it underlay half the chancel, and that there were more than a score of Mohunes lying there. It had not been opened for over forty years, since Gerald Mohune, who burst a blood-vessel drinking at Weymouth races, was buried there; but there was a tale that one Sunday afternoon, many years back, there had come from the vault so horrible and unearthly a cry, that parson and people got up and fled from the church, and would not worship there for weeks afterwards.
===Chinese===
教堂叫洪水浸得那么潮，Ratsey 师傅便在后头那只火盆里生起了火——往常总要等冬天真正开了头，那火盆才点。我们几个男孩都尽量紧挨着火盆坐，因为湿冷从石板地里一个劲儿往上冒；再说我们离牧师那么远，又叫橡木椅背遮得严严实实，烤个苹果、煨个栗子，不大怕叫人逮住。可是那天早晨另有一桩事分了我们的心：礼拜还没正经开始，我们就觉察出教堂底下有一种奇怪的响动。头一回响，正赶上 Glennie 先生念完“亲爱的弟兄们”那段开场；第二篇经课之前，我们又听见了一回。那声音并不响，倒有点像海上这条小船磕碰那条小船的动静，只是里头更沉、更空一些。我们几个男孩面面相觑，因为我们知道教堂底下是什么，也知道那声音只可能出自 Mohune 家的墓窖。Moonfleet 没有一个人见过那墓窖的里面；不过 Ratsey 听他父亲说过——他父亲在他之前就当这个差——墓窖占了半个圣坛的地底，里面躺着二十多位 Mohune 家的人。墓窖有四十多年不曾打开了，自从 Gerald Mohune 埋进去以后——他在韦茅斯赛马会上喝酒喝得爆了血管；可是有个传说，说许多年前一个礼拜天的下午，墓窖里发出过一声那么可怕、那么不像人间的喊叫，牧师和会众纷纷起身逃出教堂，之后连着好几个礼拜都不肯到那儿做礼拜。

===Original===
We thought of these stories, and huddled up closer to the brazier, being frightened at the noise, and uncertain whether we should not turn tail and run from the church. For it was certain that something was moving in the Mohune vault, to which there was no entrance except by a ringed stone in the chancel floor, that had not been lifted for forty years.
===Chinese===
我们想起这些传闻，愈发紧紧地朝火盆缩成一团，心里害怕那响声，拿不定主意是不是该掉头逃出教堂。因为千真万确，有什么东西正在 Mohune 家的墓窖里动——那墓窖没有别的入口，只有圣坛地面上那块带环的活石，而那块石头已经四十年不曾抬起过了。

===Original===
However, we thought better of it, and did not budge, though I could see when standing up and looking over the tops of the seats that others beside ourselves were ill at ease; for Granny Tucker gave such starts when she heard the sounds, that twice her spectacles fell off her nose into her lap, and Master Ratsey seemed to be trying to mask the one noise by making another himself, whether by shuffling with his feet or by thumping down his prayer book. But the thing that most surprised me was that even Elzevir Block, who cared, men said, for neither God nor Devil, looked unquiet, and gave a quick glance at Ratsey every time the sound came. So we sat till Mr. Glennie was well on with the sermon. His discourse interested me though I was only a boy, for he likened life to the letter Y, saying that “in each man’s life must come a point where two roads part like the arms of a Y, and that everyone must choose for himself whether he will follow the broad and sloping path on the left or the steep and narrow path on the right. For,” said he, “if you will look in your books, you will see that the letter Y is not like the Mohunes’, with both arms equal, but has the arm on the left broader and more sloping than the arm on the right; hence ancient philosophers hold that this arm on the left represents the easy downward road to destruction, and the arm on the right the narrow upward path of life.” When we heard that we all fell to searching our prayerbooks for a capital Y; and Granny Tucker, who knew not A from B, made much ado in fumbling with her book, for she would have people think that she could read. Then just at that moment came a noise from below louder than those before, hollow and grating like the cry of an old man in pain. With that up jumps Granny Tucker, calling out loud in church to Mr. Glennie﻿—
===Chinese===
不过我们转念一想，还是没有挪窝；只是每逢我站起来从椅背顶上望过去，看得出除我们之外，别人也坐不安稳：Tucker 老婆婆每回听见那声响，都惊跳个不住，有两回眼镜从鼻梁上跌下来，落进膝头；Ratsey 师傅呢，像是要自己弄出些动静来压过它——一会儿用脚蹭地板，一会儿把《公祷书》砰地往下一墩。最叫我吃惊的却是，连 Elzevir Block——人们说他天也不怕、地也不怕，上帝和魔鬼一概不放在心上——也露出一副心神不宁的样子，那声音每响一回，他都飞快地朝 Ratsey 瞟一眼。我们就这样坐着，直到 Glennie 先生的布道讲得入了港。他那篇讲道虽说我不过是个孩子，却听得津津有味，因为他把人生比作字母 Y，说“每个人的生命里必定来到一个处所，两条路在那里像 Y 的两条臂一样分开；走左边那条又宽又缓的路，还是走右边那条又陡又窄的路，人人都得自己拿主意。因为，”他说，“你们翻开书看看便知，字母 Y 不像 Mohune 家的那样两臂一般齐，它左边的臂比右边的更宽、更斜；所以古代的哲人认为，左臂代表通往毁灭的那条轻松的下坡路，右臂代表通往生命的那条狭窄的上坡路。”我们听了，都埋头在自己的《公祷书》里找起大写的 Y 来；连 A 和 B 都分不清的 Tucker 老婆婆也捧着书乱翻乱摸、忙作一团，因为她要人家当她识得字。正在这时，底下传来一声比先前都响的响动，空洞刺耳，像老人在剧痛中哀叫——

===Original===
“O Master, however can’ee bide there preaching when the Moons be rising from their graves?” and out from the church.
===Chinese===
“哎呀先生，Mohune 家的老祖宗们都从坟里爬起来了，你怎么还能站在那儿讲道呀？”喊着就冲出了教堂。

===Original===
That was too much for the others, and all fled, Mrs. Vining crying, “Lord-sakes, we shall all be throttled like Cracky Jones.”
===Chinese===
这一下别人再也受不住了，全都逃了出去，Vining 太太一路喊着：“老天爷，咱们都要像 Cracky Jones 那样被勒死啦。”

===Original===
So in a minute there were none left in the church, save and except Mr. Glennie, with me, Ratsey, and Elzevir Block. I did not run: first, not wishing to show myself coward before the men; second, because I thought if Blackbeard came he would fall on the men rather than on a boy; and third, that if it came to blows, Block was strong enough to give account even of a Mohune. Mr. Glennie went on with his sermon, making as though he neither heard any noise nor saw the people leave the church; and when he had finished, Elzevir walked out, but I stopped to see what the minister would say to Ratsey about the noise in the vault. The sexton helped Mr. Glennie off with his gown, and then seeing me standing by and listening, said﻿—
===Chinese===
转眼间，教堂里一个人也不剩了，除了 Glennie 先生，只剩下我、Ratsey 和 Elzevir Block。我没有跑：第一，我不想在这些大人面前显出自己是个胆小鬼；第二，我想 Blackbeard 若真的来了，他扑的也会是那几个大人，而不是一个孩子；第三，真要动起手来，Block 的力气，哪怕对付一个 Mohune 家的鬼也绰绰有余。Glennie 先生照旧讲他的道，装作既没听见响声，也没看见人们离开教堂；他讲完之后，Elzevir 走了出去，我却留下来，想听听牧师会怎样对 Ratsey 说墓窖里的响动。司事帮 Glennie 先生脱下法衣，接着看见我站在旁边听着，便说道——

===Original===
“The Lord has sent evil angels among us; ’tis a terrible thing, Master Glennie, to hear the dead men moving under our feet.”
===Chinese===
“上帝派了恶天使降到我们中间来了；Glennie 先生，听见死人就在我们脚底下走动，这可是件可怕的事啊。”

===Original===
“Tut, tut,” answered the minister, “it is only their own fears that make such noises terrible to the vulgar. As for Blackbeard, I am not here to say whether guilty spirits sometimes cannot rest and are seen wandering by men; but for these noises, they are certainly Nature’s work as is the noise of waves upon the beach. The floods have filled the vault with water, and so the coffins getting afloat, move in some eddies that we know not of, and jostle one another. Then being hollow, they give forth those sounds you hear, and these are your evil angels. ’Tis very true the dead do move beneath our feet, but ’tis because they cannot help themselves, being carried hither and thither by the water. Fie, Ratsey man, you should know better than to fright a boy with silly talk of spirits when the truth is bad enough.”
===Chinese===
“嗐，嗐，”牧师答道，“这样的响动在俗人听来可怕，不过是他们自己心里的恐惧作祟。至于 Blackbeard，有罪的鬼魂是不是有时候不得安息、被人看见四处游荡，这不归我来说；可要说这些响动，它们千真万确是大自然的作为，就像海滩上的涛声一样。洪水把墓窖灌满了水，棺材便浮了起来，在一些我们不知道的漩涡里漂移，彼此磕磕碰碰。它们既然是空膛的，就发出你们听见的那种声音——这就是你们那些恶天使。不错，死人确实在我们脚底下动，可那是因为他们自己做不得主，叫水冲过来又冲过去。呸，Ratsey 老兄，实情已经够糟的了，你还拿神神鬼鬼的蠢话去吓唬一个孩子，真该懂得分寸些。”

===Original===
The parson’s words had the ring of truth in them to me, and I never doubted that he was right. So this mystery was explained, and yet it was a dreadful thing, and made me shiver, to think of the Mohunes all adrift in their coffins, and jostling one another in the dark. I pictured them to myself, the many generations, old men and children, man and maid, all bones now, each afloat in his little box of rotting wood; and Blackbeard himself in a great coffin bigger than all the rest, coming crashing into the weaker ones, as a ship in a heavy sea comes crashing down sometimes in the trough, on a small boat that is trying to board her. And then there was the outer darkness of the vault itself to think of, and the close air, and the black putrid water nearly up to the roof on which such sorry ships were sailing.
===Chinese===
在我听来，牧师的话句句透着在理，我从没怀疑过他说得不对。这个谜就这样解开了；可是想到 Mohune 一家全在棺材里漂着，在黑暗里你撞我、我撞你，终究是件可怕的事，叫我不寒而栗。我在心里描画他们：一代又一代的人，老人和孩子，男人和姑娘，如今都成了白骨，各自漂在自己那口朽木小匣子里；Blackbeard 本尊躺在一口比谁都大的棺材里，一路撞向那些不结实的——就像大浪里的巨船，有时从浪谷里当头砸下来，砸在正要攀登它的小船上。再想想墓窖本身那外层的黑暗，那窒闷的空气，那几乎涨到拱顶、载着这一支凄惨船队的黑臭死水。

===Original===
Ratsey looked a little crestfallen at what Mr. Glennie said, but put a good face on it, and answered﻿—
===Chinese===
Glennie 先生这番话说得 Ratsey 有几分蔫头耷脑，可他还是强撑着脸面，答道——

===Original===
“Well, master, I am but a plain man, and know nothing about floods and these eddies and hidden workings of Nature of which you speak; but, saving your presence, I hold it a fond thing to make light of such warnings as are given us. ’Tis always said, ‘When the Moons move, then Moonfleet mourns’; and I have heard my father tell that the last time they stirred was in Queen Anne’s second year, when the great storm blew men’s homes about their heads. And as for frighting children, ’tis well that heady boys should learn to stand in awe, and not pry into what does not concern them﻿—or they may come to harm.” He added the last words with what I felt sure was a nod of warning to myself, though I did not then understand what he meant. So he walked off in a huff with Elzevir, who was waiting for him outside, and I went with Mr. Glennie and carried his gown for him back to his lodging in the village.
===Chinese===
“唉，先生，我不过是个粗人，什么洪水啊，您说的这些漩涡啊、大自然暗地里的作为啊，我一概不懂；可是恕我直言，把人家给我们的这等警兆不当回事，我看是桩蠢事。老话向来这么说：‘Mohune 家一挪窝，Moonfleet 就遭祸’；我听我父亲讲过，他们上一回动弹，正是在安妮女王二年，那场大风把人们的房子都掀塌在他们头顶上。至于吓唬孩子——愣头愣脑的男孩正该学着存点敬畏，别去乱探与自己不相干的事——不然是要吃亏的。”末了那几句话，他说的时候朝我点了点头，我敢肯定那是对我的警告，只是当时不明白他是什么意思。于是他同在外面等他的 Elzevir 怒气冲冲地走了，我陪着 Glennie 先生，替他抱着法衣，送他回村里的住处去。

===Original===
Mr. Glennie was always very friendly, making much of me, and talking to me as though I were his equal; which was due, I think, to there being no one of his own knowledge in the neighbourhood, and so he had as lief talk to an ignorant boy as to an ignorant man. After we had passed the churchyard turnstile and were crossing the sludgy meadows, I asked him again what he knew of Blackbeard and his lost treasure.
===Chinese===
Glennie 先生待人一向非常和气，很看得起我，同我说话就像同平辈人一样；我想，这是因为左近没有一个谈得上他有学问的人，所以同一个无知的男人谈天也好，同一个无知的孩子谈天也好，在他都一样。走过教堂墓园的旋转栅门、蹚着泥泞的草甸时，我又问起他，他所知道的 Blackbeard 和那件失落的财宝到底是怎么回事。

===Original===
“My son,” he answered, “all that I have been able to gather is, that this Colonel John Mohune (foolishly called Blackbeard) was the first to impair the family fortunes by his excesses, and even let the almshouses fall to ruin, and turned the poor away. Unless report strangely belies him, he was an evil man, and besides numberless lesser crimes, had on his hands the blood of a faithful servant, whom he made away with because chance had brought to the man’s ears some guilty secret of the master. Then, at the end of his life, being filled with fear and remorse (as must always happen with evil livers at the last), he sent for Rector Kindersley of Dorchester to confess him, though a Protestant, and wished to make amends by leaving that treasure so ill-gotten from King Charles (which was all that he had to leave) for the repair and support of the almshouses. He made a last will, which I have seen, to this effect, but without describing the treasure further than to call it a diamond, nor saying where it was to be found. Doubtless he meant to get it himself, sell it, and afterwards apply the profit to his good purpose, but before he could do so death called him suddenly to his account. So men say that he cannot rest in his grave, not having made even so tardy a reparation, and never will rest unless the treasure is found and spent upon the poor.”
===Chinese===
“孩子，”他答道，“我所能打听到的只是这些：这位 John Mohune 上校（人们蠢笨地管他叫 Blackbeard）是头一个纵欲挥霍、伤了家业元气的人，连济贫院都任它败落，把穷人们赶了出去。除非传闻大大冤枉了他，他是个恶人，且不说数不清的小罪过，他手上还沾着一个忠实仆人的血——那人不过碰巧把主人某桩罪恶的秘密听了去，就被他结果了性命。到了晚年，他心里充满恐惧和悔恨（作恶的人临到末了必定如此），便派人去请多切斯特的 Kindersley 教区长来听他忏悔——尽管那是一位新教徒——又想立下那件从查理国王手里昧来的不义之财（这也是他仅有的可留之物），用来修缮和维持济贫院，以此赎罪。他立了一份遗嘱，我也见过，大意如此；只是对那件财宝，除了称它是一颗钻石，再没有别的描述，也没有说它藏在什么地方。毫无疑问，他打算自己把它取出来，卖掉，再把所得用在他那桩善举上；可是还没来得及办，死亡就突然把他唤去清账了。所以人们说，他在坟墓里不得安息，因为连这样迟来的一步补偿都不曾做成；而且那财宝不找出来、不花在穷人身上，他就永远不得安息。”

===Original===
I thought much over what Mr. Glennie had said and fell to wondering where Blackbeard could have hid his diamond, and whether I might not find it some day and make myself a rich man. Now, as I considered that noise we had heard under the church, and Parson Glennie’s explanation of it, I was more and more perplexed; for the noise had, as I have said, something deep and hollow-booming in it, and how was that to be made by decayed coffins? I had more than once seen Ratsey, in digging a grave, turn up pieces of coffins, and sometimes a tarnished nameplate would show that they had not been so very long underground, and yet the wood was quite decayed and rotten. And granting that such were in the earth, and so might more easily perish, yet when the top was taken off old Guy’s brick grave to put his widow beside him, Master Ratsey gave me a peep in, and old Guy’s coffin had cracks and warps in it, and looked as if a sound blow would send it to pieces. Yet here were the Mohune coffins that had been put away for generations, and must be rotten as tinder, tapping against each other with a sound like a drum, as if they were still sound and airtight. Still, Mr. Glennie must be right; for if it was not the coffins, what should it be that made the noise?
===Chinese===
我把 Glennie 先生的话想了又想，渐渐琢磨起 Blackbeard 会把他的钻石藏在哪儿，我有没有哪一天找到它、变成个有钱人的福分。可是，一想到我们在教堂底下听见的那阵响动，一想到 Glennie 牧师对它的解释，我就越想越糊涂；因为那声音，正像我说的，里头带着一种深沉空阔的嗡响，朽烂的棺材怎么发得出这样的声音呢？我不止一次见 Ratsey 挖坟时翻出棺材板来，有时一块发乌的铭牌还表明它们入土并不很久，木头却已经朽得一塌糊涂。就算那些是埋在土里、烂得更快吧，可是给老 Guy 的砖砌坟墓揭顶、要安葬他的寡妇的时候，Ratsey 师傅让我探头往里瞧过一眼：老 Guy 的棺材已经满是裂缝和翘弯，仿佛结结实实一拳就能打得它粉碎。可眼前这些 Mohune 家的棺材，入土已经隔了好几代，必定烂得像火绒了，彼此磕碰出来的声音却像擂鼓，倒仿佛它们还结结实实、密不透气似的。不过，Glennie 先生总归是对的；因为若不是棺材，那声音又能是什么弄出来的呢？

===Original===
So on the next day after we heard the sounds in church, being the Monday, as soon as morning school was over, off I ran down street and across meadows to the churchyard, meaning to listen outside the church if the Mohunes were still moving. I say outside the church, for I knew Ratsey would not lend me the key to go in after what he had said about boys prying into things that did not concern them; and besides that, I do not know that I should care to have ventured inside alone, even if I had the key.
===Chinese===
于是，在教堂里听见那响动的第二天——那天是礼拜一——早课一散，我就顺着街道一路跑下去，穿过草甸直奔教堂墓园，打算在教堂外面听听，Mohune 家的人还在不在动。我说在外面，是因为我知道，听了 Ratsey 那番男孩子不该乱探不相干之事的话，他是决不会把钥匙借给我进去的；再说，就算有钥匙，我一个人独个儿闯进去，只怕我也未必敢。

===Original===
When I reached the church, not a little out of breath, I listened first on the side nearest the village, that is the north side; putting my ear against the wall, and afterwards lying down on the ground, though the grass was long and wet, so that I might the better catch any sound that came. But I could hear nothing, and so concluded that the Mohunes had come to rest again, yet thought I would walk round the church and listen too on the south or sea side, for that their worships might have drifted over to that side, and be there rubbing shoulders with one another. So I went round, and was glad to get out of the cold shade into the sun on the south. But here was a surprise; for when I came round a great buttress which juts out from the wall, what should I see but two men, and these two were Ratsey and Elzevir Block. I came upon them unawares, and, lo and behold, there was Master Ratsey lying also on the ground with his ear to the wall, while Elzevir sat back against the inside of the buttress with a spyglass in his hand, smoking and looking out to sea.
===Chinese===
跑到教堂跟前，我已经上气不接下气。我先在离村子最近的一面，也就是北面，听了一阵：把耳朵贴在墙上，后来索性趴到地上——草又长又湿也顾不得——好把传出来的响动听得更真切些。可是什么也听不见，于是断定 Mohune 家的人又安生了；不过我想，还是绕到教堂南面、也就是临海的那一面去听听才好，说不定那几位老大人已经漂到了那边，正在那儿摩肩接踵呢。我便绕了过去，走出背阴的寒气，晒上南面的太阳，心里正自高兴。谁知这里有一桩意外：我刚绕过从墙上凸出来的一道大扶壁，一眼看见两个人——不是别人，正是 Ratsey 和 Elzevir Block。我冷不丁撞见了他们；你瞧，Ratsey 师傅竟也趴在地上，耳朵贴着墙，Elzevir 则背靠着扶壁的内侧，手里拿着一架望远镜，一边抽烟，一边望着大海。

===Original===
Now, I had as much right to be in the churchyard as Ratsey or Elzevir, and yet I felt a sudden shame as if I had been caught in some bad act, and knew the blood was running to my cheeks. At first I had it in my mind to turn tail and make off, but concluded to stand my ground since they had seen me, and so bade them “Good morning.” Master Ratsey jumped to his feet as nimbly as a cat; and if he had not been a man, I should have thought he was blushing too, for his face was very red, though that came perhaps from lying on the ground. I could see he was a little put about, and out of countenance, though he tried to say “Good morning, John,” in an easy tone, as if it was a common thing for him to be lying in the churchyard, with his ear to the wall, on a winter’s morning. “Good morning, John,” he said; “and what might you be doing in the churchyard this fine day?”
===Chinese===
说起来，我站在教堂墓园里的权利，同 Ratsey 和 Elzevir 一模一样；可我还是猛地觉出一阵羞惭，仿佛干什么坏事叫人当场拿住了，只觉得血直往脸上涌。起初我想掉头就溜，转念一想，反正他们已经瞧见我了，还是站稳脚跟的好，便向他们道了一声“早上好”。Ratsey 师傅一纵身就跳了起来，敏捷得像只猫；他若不是个男子汉，我准以为他也在脸红呢，因为他那张脸红得很——不过那也许是趴在地上趴出来的。我看得出他有点儿发窘，有点儿下不来台，虽然他竭力拿轻快的口气说“早上好，John”，仿佛大冬天的早晨趴在墓园里、耳朵贴着墙，在他本是稀松平常的事。“早上好，John，”他说，“这么个好天儿，你到墓园里来做什么呀？”

===Original===
I answered that I was come to listen if the Mohunes were still moving.
===Chinese===
我回答说，我是来听听 Mohune 家的人还动不动的。

===Original===
“Well, that I can’t tell you,” returned Ratsey, “not wishing to waste thought on such idle matters, and having to examine this wall whether the floods have not so damaged it as to need underpinning; so if you have time to gad about of a morning, get you back to my workshop and fetch me a plasterer’s hammer which I have left behind, so that I can try this mortar.”
===Chinese===
“唔，这我可说不上，”Ratsey 答道，“这种没要紧的闲事，我犯不上费心思；我还得查看查看这道墙，看洪水把它毁坏到什么地步，用不用加固墙基。你要是大清早有工夫闲逛，就替我回趟工棚，把我落在那儿的泥瓦匠小锤取来，我好试试这灰浆。”

===Original===
I knew that he was making excuses about underpinning, for the wall was sound as a rock, but was glad enough to take him at his word and beat a retreat from where I was not wanted. Indeed, I soon saw how he was mocking me, for the men did not even wait for me to come back with the hammer, but I met them returning in the first meadow. Master Ratsey made another excuse that he did not need the hammer now, as he had found out that all that was wanted was a little pointing with new mortar. “But if you have such time to waste, John,” he added, “you can come tomorrow and help me to get new thwarts in the *Petrel*, which she badly wants.”
===Chinese===
我知道他加固墙基云云不过是找托词，因为那道墙结实得像岩石；不过我巴不得就着他这话，从人家不欢迎我的地方体面撤退。果然，我很快就看出他是怎么捉弄我的：他们压根儿没等我取锤子回来，我在头一块草甸上就迎面碰上了他们往回走。Ratsey 师傅又找了个借口，说如今用不着锤子了，因为他发现要做的，无非是用新灰浆勾一勾缝。“不过，John，你要是真有那么多闲工夫白糟蹋，”他补了一句，“明天就来帮我给‘Petrel’号换几块新座板吧，这活儿它正等着要呢。”

===Original===
So we three came back to the village together; but looking up at Elzevir once while Master Ratsey was making these pretences, I saw his eyes twinkle under their heavy brows, as if he was amused at the other’s embarrassment.
===Chinese===
于是我们三个一道回了村；只是当 Ratsey 师傅编派这些托词的时候，我抬头看了 Elzevir 一眼，只见他浓眉底下目光闪闪，仿佛在笑对方的窘态。

===Original===
The next Sunday, when we went to church, all was quiet as usual, there was no Elzevir, and no more noises, and I never heard the Mohunes move again.
===Chinese===
下一个礼拜天我们去做礼拜，一切同往常一样安静：Elzevir 没有来，响声也没有了，而且打那以后，我再没有听见 Mohune 家的人动过。

## Epigraph / 题词

===Original===
Says the Cap’n to the Crew,
					

					We have slipped the Revenue,
					

					I can see the cliffs of Dover on the lee:
					

					Tip the signal to the *Swan*,
					

					And anchor broadside on,
					

					And out with the kegs of Eau-de-Vie,
					

					Says the Cap’n:
					

					Out with the kegs of Eau-de-Vie.

Says the Lander to his men,
					

					Get your grummets on the pin,
					

					There’s a blue light burning out at sea.
					

					The windward anchors creep,
					

					And the Gauger’s fast asleep,
					

					And the kegs are bobbing one, two, three,
					

					Says the Lander:
					

					The kegs are bobbing one, two, three.

But the bold Preventive man
					

					Primes the powder in his pan
					

					And cries to the Posse, Follow me.
					

					We will take this smuggling gang,
					

					And those that fight shall hang
					

					Dingle dangle from the execution tree,
					

					Says the Gauger:
					

					Dingle dangle with the weary moon to see.
===Chinese===
船长对水手们说道：

我们已甩开了缉私船，

多佛的悬崖正在下风一面：

给*天鹅号*打出信号，

抛锚横舷停靠，

把白兰地酒桶搬出来，

船长说道：

把白兰地酒桶搬出来。

接货人对伙伴们说道：

把索环套上销钉，

海面上燃起了蓝色信号灯。

上风锚悄悄爬动，

税务官睡意正浓，

酒桶颠簸着一、二、三，

接货人说道：

酒桶颠簸着一、二、三。

可那大胆的缉私队员

往火药池里装好火药，

招呼队勇们：跟我来。

我们要拿下这帮走私贩，

胆敢顽抗的统统绞死，

在绞刑树上晃晃荡荡，

税务官说道：

晃晃荡荡，还有倦月相看。

## III A Discovery / 三 一项发现

===Original===
“Some bold adventurers disdain
						

						The limits of their little reign,
						

						And unknown regions dare descry;
						

						Still, as they run, they look behind,
						

						They hear a voice in every wind
						

						And snatch a fearful joy.”

> Gray
===Chinese===
“有些大胆的冒险家，鄙夷
						自己小小王国的疆界，
						敢把未知的疆域窥探；
						他们一路奔逃，仍频频回顾，
						听见每一阵风里有声息，
						攫住一份战栗的欢愉。”

> Gray

===Original===
I have said that I used often in the daytime, when not at school, to go to the churchyard, because being on a little rise, there was the best view of the sea to be had from it; and on a fine day you could watch the French privateers creeping along the cliffs under the Snout, and lying in wait for an Indiaman or up-channel trader. There were at Moonfleet few boys of my own age, and none that I cared to make my companion; so I was given to muse alone, and did so for the most part in the open air, all the more because my aunt did not like to see an idle boy, with muddy boots, about her house.
===Chinese===
我已说过，白天不上学的时候，我常到教堂墓园去，因为它坐落在一片小小的隆起上，从那里望海眼界最好；天气晴好的日子，你能望见法国的私掠船贴着 Snout 海岬底下的峭壁悄悄游弋，守候着印度商船，或是溯海峡北上的商船。Moonfleet 同我年纪相仿的男孩本就没有几个，也没有一个我乐意引为同伴；于是我惯于独自想心事，而且多半是在露天里想，何况姑母本就不爱见一个靴子上带泥、闲着没事的男孩在她家里转悠。

===Original===
For a few weeks, indeed, after the day that I had surprised Elzevir and Ratsey, I kept away from the church, fearing to meet them there again; but a little later resumed my visits, and saw no more of them. Now, my favourite seat in the churchyard was the flat top of a raised stone tomb, which stands on the southeast of the church. I have heard Mr. Glennie call it an altar-tomb, and in its day it had been a fine monument, being carved round with festoons of fruit and flowers; but had suffered so much from the weather, that I never was able to read the lettering on it, or to find out who had been buried beneath. Here I chose most to sit, not only because it had a flat and convenient top, but because it was screened from the wind by a thick clump of yew-trees. These yews had once, I think, completely surrounded it, but had either died or been cut down on the south side, so that anyone sitting on the grave-top was snug from the weather, and yet possessed a fine prospect over the sea. On the other three sides, the yews grew close and thick, embowering the tomb like the high back of a fireside chair; and many times in autumn I have seen the stone slab crimson with the fallen waxy berries, and taken some home to my aunt, who liked to taste them with a glass of sloe-gin after her Sunday dinner. Others beside me, no doubt, found this tomb a comfortable seat and lookout; for there was quite a path worn to it on the south side, though all the times I had visited it I had never seen anyone there.
===Chinese===
的确，自从那天撞见了 Elzevir 和 Ratsey，我有几个礼拜躲着教堂没去，生怕再在那里碰上他们；可是过了些日子，我又照旧去了，再没见着他们的踪影。我在墓园里最爱坐的位子，是一座高基石棺的平顶，它立在教堂的东南面。我听 Glennie 先生管这种坟叫祭坛墓；想当年它也是座体面的纪念碑，四周雕着一串串果子花朵的垂花饰；只是叫风雨糟蹋得太狠，我从没能辨认出上面的刻字，也没打听出底下埋的是谁。我最爱挑这里坐，不只因为它顶面平整合用，还因为有一丛茂密的紫杉替它挡风。这些紫杉，我想，从前原是把坟四面围拢的，只是南面的要么枯死了、要么叫人砍了去，于是坐在墓顶上的人既避得开风雨，又占得一片望海的好景致。另外三面的紫杉长得密密层层，把坟墓拢在当中，好像壁炉边座椅那高高的椅背；多少个秋天，我看见石板叫落下的蜡质浆果染得通红，还拾过一些带回给姑母——她喜欢在礼拜天正餐之后，就着一杯黑刺李杜松子酒尝上几粒。不必说，除了我，别人想必也觉得这座坟是个舒坦的座处和瞭望台；因为南面分明叫人踩出了一条直达坟前的小径，虽然我去了那么些回，从没在那里遇上过一个人。

===Original===
So it came about that on a certain afternoon in the beginning of February, in the year 1758, I was sitting on this tomb looking out to sea. Though it was so early in the year, the air was soft and warm as a May day, and so still that I could hear the drumming of turnips that Gaffer George was flinging into a cart on the hillside, near half a mile away. Ever since the floods of which I have spoken, the weather had been open, but with high winds, and little or no rain. Thus as the land dried after the floods there began to open cracks in the heavy clay soil on which Moonfleet is built, such as are usually only seen with us in the height of summer. There were cracks by the side of the path in the sea-meadows between the village and the church, and cracks in the churchyard itself, and one running right up to this very tomb.
===Chinese===
就这样，到了 1758 年二月初的某个下午，我正坐在这座坟上望海。虽说年头还这么早，空气却柔和暖和得同五月天一样，又那么静谧，静得连山坡上 Gaffer George 老汉往大车里扔萝卜的咚咚声我都听得见——那地方离这里差不多半英里。自我说起过的那场洪水以来，天气一直敞亮，只是风大，雨却下得很少，或者压根儿没下。于是大水之后地面渐渐干缩，Moonfleet 村所坐落的那片黏重泥土里，开始绽开一道道裂缝——这样的裂缝，在我们这里寻常只有盛夏才见得着。村子同教堂之间的海边草甸里，小路两旁裂了缝，墓园里也裂了缝，还有一道，恰恰一直裂到这座坟跟前。

===Original===
It must have been past four o’clock in the afternoon, and I was for returning to tea at my aunt’s, when underneath the stone on which I sat I heard a rumbling and crumbling, and on jumping off saw that the crack in the ground had still further widened, just where it came up to the tomb, and that the dry earth had so shrunk and settled that there was a hole in the ground a foot or more across. Now this hole reached under the big stone that formed one side of the tomb, and falling on my hands and knees and looking down it, I perceived that there was under the monument a larger cavity, into which the hole opened. I believe there never was boy yet who saw a hole in the ground, or a cave in a hill, or much more an underground passage, but longed incontinently to be into it and discover whither it led. So it was with me; and seeing that the earth had fallen enough into the hole to open a way under the stone, I slipped myself in feet foremost, dropped down on to a heap of fallen mould, and found that I could stand upright under the monument itself.
===Chinese===
那时想必已过下午四点，我正打算回姑母家吃茶点，忽然听见身子底下的石头里发出一阵轰隆塌落的响动；跳下来一看，只见地上的裂缝恰在贴到坟墓的地方又展宽了许多，干土收缩沉陷得那么厉害，地上竟豁出一个洞，一英尺多宽。这洞正通到砌成坟墓一侧的那块大石头底下；我扑下去双手双膝着地，朝洞里一望，察觉纪念碑底下有一个更大的空腔，这个洞便通向那里头。我相信，天底下从来不曾有过一个男孩，见了地上的洞、山里的穴，更不必说地下的暗道，而不登时心痒难熬、想要钻进去探个水落石出的。我自然也是一样；眼见塌下去的土已在石头底下让出一条路，我便脚在前头，缩身钻了进去，落到一堆塌土上，发觉自己竟能在纪念碑底下直挺挺地站着。

===Original===
Now this was what I had expected, for I thought that there had been below this grave a vault, the roof of which had given way and let the earth fall in. But as soon as my eyes were used to the dimmer light, I saw that it was no such thing, but that the hole into which I had crept was only the mouth of a passage, which sloped gently down in the direction of the church. My heart fell to thumping with eagerness and surprise, for I thought I had made a wonderful discovery, and that this hidden way would certainly lead to great things, perhaps even to Blackbeard’s hoard; for ever since Mr. Glennie’s tale I had constantly before my eyes a vision of the diamond and the wealth it was to bring me. The passage was two paces broad, as high as a tall man, and cut through the soil, without bricks or any other lining; and what surprised me most was that it did not seem deserted nor mouldy and cobwebbed, as one would expect such a place to be, but rather a well-used thoroughfare; for I could see the soft clay floor was trodden with the prints of many boots, and marked with a trail as if some heavy thing had been dragged over it.
===Chinese===
这本在我意料之中，因为我想，这座坟底下该是有一个墓窖，窖顶塌了陷，土才跟着落了下去。可是等眼睛习惯了昏暗，我看出根本不是那么回事：我爬进来的这个洞，不过是一条通道的口子，那通道缓缓朝教堂的方向斜下去。我的心咚咚地急跳起来，又热切又惊奇，因为我觉得自己做成了一桩了不得的大发现，这条隐秘的路必定通向了不得的去处，说不定就通到 Blackbeard 的窖藏；因为自听了 Glennie 先生讲的那段故事，我眼前便时时浮动着那颗钻石，和它将给我带来的财富。那通道有两步宽，一个高个子男人那么高，是从泥土里掏挖出来的，不砌砖，也没有别的衬砌；最叫我惊奇的是，它不见荒废，也不像这种地方该有的那样发霉结网，倒像一条常有人走的通路；因为我看得出，松软的泥地面上踩满了许许多多靴子的印子，还有一道拖痕，仿佛有什么重家伙从上面拖运过去。

===Original===
So I set out down the passage, reaching out my hand before me lest I should run against anything in the dark, and sliding my feet slowly to avoid pitfalls in the floor. But before I had gone half a dozen paces, the darkness grew so black that I was frightened, and so far from going on was glad to turn sharp about, and see the glimmer of light that came in through the hole under the tomb. Then a horror of the darkness seized me, and before I well knew what I was about I found myself wriggling my body up under the tombstone on to the churchyard grass, and was once more in the low evening sunlight and the soft sweet air.
===Chinese===
于是我顺着通道往下走，把手伸在身前，怕在黑暗里撞上什么，两脚贴着地面慢慢挪，防着地上有陷坑。可是没走上十来步，黑暗浓得那么厉害，我怕了，哪里还谈得上前进，只巴不得猛地转过身去，好望着坟墓底下洞口透进来的那一星微光。跟着，一阵对黑暗的恐怖攫住了我，还没等我明白自己在做什么，已经扭动着身子从墓碑底下爬了上去，趴到墓园的草地上，重新回到西斜的太阳和柔软香甜的空气里。

===Original===
Home I ran to my aunt’s, for it was past teatime, and beside that I knew I must fetch a candle if I were ever to search out the passage; and to search it I had well made up my mind, no matter how much I was scared for this moment. My aunt gave me but a sorry greeting when I came into the kitchen, for I was late and hot. She never said much when displeased, but had a way of saying nothing, which was much worse; and would only reply yes or no, and that after an interval, to anything that was asked of her. So the meal was silent enough, for she had finished before I arrived, and I ate but little myself, being too much occupied with the thought of my strange discovery, and finding, besides, the tea lukewarm and the victuals not enticing.
===Chinese===
我一路跑回姑母家，因为吃茶点的时辰已经过了；再说我知道，真要探明那条通道，总得取一支蜡烛；而探明它，我是拿定了主意的，哪怕这会儿吓成这样也罢。我进厨房时，姑母给我的招待可够冷淡，因为我回来晚了，又是一身燥热。她不高兴时从来不多说话，却有一套一言不发的做派，那比骂人还难受；问她什么，她只隔上好一阵，才答一个是或不。这一顿饭于是吃得够沉闷，她在我回来前早已吃完；我自己也吃得很少，一脑子全是我那桩奇怪的发现，何况茶是温吞的，饭菜也不馋人。

===Original===
You may guess that I said nothing of what I had seen, but made up my mind that as soon as my aunt’s back was turned I would get a candle and tinderbox, and return to the churchyard. The sun was down before Aunt Jane gave thanks for what we had received, and then, turning to me, she said in a cold and measured voice:
===Chinese===
诸位猜得到，我看见的事我一个字也没有提，只打定主意，等姑母一转背，就去取一支蜡烛和火绒盒，重回墓园去。直到太阳落了山，Jane 姑妈才为领受的饮食做了谢恩祷告；随后她转向我，用一种冷冷的、字斟句酌的腔调说：

===Original===
“John, I have observed that you are often out and about of nights, sometimes as late as half past seven or eight. Now, it is not seemly for young folk to be abroad after dark, and I do not choose that my nephew should be called a gadabout. ‘What’s bred in the bone will come out in the flesh,’ and ’twas with such loafing that your father began his wild ways, and afterwards led my poor sister such a life as never was, till the mercy of Providence took him away.”
===Chinese===
“John，我留意到，你夜里时常在外头逛荡，有时候竟晚到七点半、八点钟。年轻人天黑以后还在外头跑，这可不体面；我不愿意让人管我的侄子叫游手好闲的二流子。‘骨子里生就的性子，皮肉上迟早要露出来’，你父亲当年就是从这般闲逛起步，走上了荒唐路，后来叫我那可怜的姊妹吃足了苦，那样日子世上再没有第二个女人受过，直到上天的慈悲把他收了去。”

===Original===
Aunt Jane often spoke thus of my father, whom I never remembered, but believe him to have been an honest man and good fellow to boot, if something given to roaming and to the contraband.
===Chinese===
Jane 姑妈时常这样说起我父亲；我对他毫无记忆，却相信他为人诚实，还是个好性子的人，只是有些流荡的脾性，又沾着走私的路子。

===Original===
“So understand,” she went on, “that I will not have you out again this evening, no, nor any other evening, after dusk. Bed is the place for youth when night falls, but if this seem to you too early you can sit with me for an hour in the parlour, and I will read you a discourse of Doctor Sherlock that will banish vain thoughts, and leave you in a fit frame for quiet sleep.”
===Chinese===
“所以你要明白，”她接着说，“今天晚上我不许你再出去；不但今晚，往后任凭哪个晚上，天一擦黑都不许。夜幕一落，年轻人就该上床；你若嫌这太早，可以到前厅陪我坐一个钟头，我给你念一篇 Sherlock 博士的讲道文，把那些胡思乱想统统念跑，叫你安安分分地睡个好觉。”

===Original===
So she led the way into the parlour, took the book from the shelf, put it on the table within the little circle of light cast by a shaded candle, and began. It was dull enough, though I had borne such tribulations before, and the drone of my aunt’s voice would have sent me to sleep, as it had done at other times, even in a straight-backed chair, had I not been so full of my discovery, and chafed at this delay. Thus all the time my aunt read of spiritualities and saving grace, I had my mind on diamonds and all kinds of mammon, for I never doubted that Blackbeard’s treasure would be found at the end of that secret passage. The sermon finished at last, and my aunt closed the book with a stiff “goodnight” for me. I was for giving her my formal kiss, but she made as if she did not see me and turned away; so we went upstairs each to our own room, and I never kissed Aunt Jane again.
===Chinese===
于是她领头进了前厅，从架上取下书，摊在带罩蜡烛投下的一小圈光里，念了起来。那篇东西枯燥得很，虽说这类磨难我从前也领教过；姑母那单调的嗡嗡声若在平时，早把我送进梦乡了——从前就是这样，连直背椅上也照睡不误——无奈我心里满满当当装着那桩发现，又为这场耽搁焦躁难耐。于是姑母念的尽是灵性和救恩，我心里想的却尽是钻石和各色各样的浮财，因为我从不怀疑，Blackbeard 的财宝就在那条秘道的尽头。讲道总算念完，姑母合上书，硬邦邦地赏了我一声“晚安”。我想照例吻她一下，她却装作没瞧见我，扭过脸去；于是我们上楼，各回各屋——从那以后，我再没有吻过 Jane 姑妈。

===Original===
There was a moon three-quarters full already in the sky, and on moonlight nights I was allowed no candle to show me to bed. But on that night I needed none, for I never took off my clothes, having resolved to wait till my aunt was asleep, and then, ghosts or no ghosts, to make my way back to the churchyard. I did not dare to put off that visit even till the morning, lest some chance passerby should light upon the hole, and so forestall me with Blackbeard’s treasure.
===Chinese===
天上已经升起一轮圆了四分之三的月亮；有月光的夜里，照例不许我点蜡烛照着上楼。可是那一夜我用不着，因为我压根儿没脱衣裳，只打定主意等姑母睡熟，然后——有鬼没鬼——摸回墓园去。这一趟我连推迟到第二天早晨都不敢，生怕哪个过路人碰巧撞上那个洞，抢在头里把 Blackbeard 的财宝得了去。

===Original===
Thus I lay wide awake on my bed watching the shadow of the tester-post against the whitewashed wall, and noting how it had moved, by degrees, as the moon went farther round. At last, just as it touched the picture of the Good Shepherd which hung over the mantelpiece, I heard my aunt snoring in her room, and knew that I was free. Yet I waited a few minutes so that she might get well on with her first sleep, and then took off my boots, and in stockinged feet slipped past her room and down the stairs. How stair, handrail, and landing creaked that night, and how my feet and body struck noisily against things seen quite well but misjudged in the effort not to misjudge them! And yet there was the note of safety still sounding, for the snoring never ceased, and the sleeper woke not, though her waking then might have changed all my life. So I came safely to the kitchen, and there put in my pocket one of the best winter candles and the tinderbox, and as I crept out of the room heard suddenly how loud the old clock was ticking, and looking up saw the bright brass band marking half past ten on the dial.
===Chinese===
就这样，我在床上睁着眼躺着，望着床架天盖的柱子投在刷白墙上的影子，看着它随着月亮越转越远，一分一分地挪动。末了，那影子恰好触到壁炉架上挂的那幅《好牧人》图，我听见姑母屋里打起呼噜来，知道自己自由了。可我还等了几分钟，好叫她头一觉睡沉，然后脱下靴子，穿着袜子蹑手蹑脚溜过她的房门，下了楼。那一夜，楼梯、扶手和楼梯口的地板响得多么厉害；我的脚和身子，又怎样一次次磕在那些看得清清楚楚、只因生怕碰错反而撞了上去的东西上，声响多么大！然而平安的底音始终没有断：呼噜一直没停，睡着的人也没有醒——虽然她那时若是醒了，我这一生的路数怕就要全盘改写。就这样，我平安到了厨房，往口袋里揣了一支上好的过冬蜡烛，又拿上火绒盒；正蹑着脚往外走，忽然听见那座老钟滴答得多么响，抬头一看，钟面上明晃晃的黄铜指针正指着十点半。

===Original===
Out in the street I kept in the shadow of the houses as far as I might, though all was silent as the grave; indeed, I think that when the moon is bright a great hush falls always upon Nature, as though she was taken up in wondering at her own beauty. Everyone was fast asleep in Moonfleet and there was no light in any window; only when I came opposite the Why Not? I saw from the red glow behind the curtains that the bottom room was lit up, so Elzevir was not yet gone to bed. It was strange, for the Why Not? had been shut up early for many a long night past, and I crossed over cautiously to see if I could make out what was going forward. But that was not to be done, for the panes were thickly steamed over; and this surprised me more as showing that there was a good company inside. Moreover, as I stood and listened I could hear a mutter of deep voices inside, not as of roisterers, but of sober men talking low.
===Chinese===
到了街上，我尽量拣房影里走，虽然四下静得像坟墓；说真的，我想，月色明亮的时候，大自然总要罩上一层深深的静谧，仿佛她自己也对着自己的美出了神。Moonfleet 家家睡得正沉，哪扇窗里都没有灯；只是走到“Why Not?”对面时，我看见窗帘后头透出红光，楼底下的屋子亮着——这么说，Elzevir 还没上床。这可蹊跷，因为许多个长夜以来，“Why Not?”总是早早便打了烊；我小心地穿过街去，想弄明白底下在搞什么名堂。可那没法办到，因为一块块窗玻璃都叫水汽蒙得严严实实；这更叫我吃惊，因为这表明里头坐着好些人。再者，我站着听的工夫，听得见里面透出一阵低沉的语声，不像起哄的酒客，倒像一群稳重的人压低了嗓子谈话。

===Original===
Eagerness would not let me wait long, and I was off across the meadows towards the church, though not without sad misgivings as soon as the last house was left well behind me. At the churchyard wall my courage had waned somewhat: it seemed a shameless thing to come to rifle Blackbeard’s treasure just in the very place and hour that Blackbeard loved; and as I passed the turnstile I half-expected that a tall figure, hairy and evil-eyed, would spring out from the shadow on the north side of the church. But nothing stirred, and the frosty grass sounded crisp under my feet as I made across the churchyard, stepping over the graves and keeping always out of the shadows, towards the black clump of yew-trees on the far side.
===Chinese===
那股急切劲儿不容我久等，我便穿起草甸往教堂去了；只是等最后一座房子远远撇在身后，心里就不能不犯起凄惶来。走到墓园墙边，我的胆量已经泄了几分：拣的偏偏是 Blackbeard 喜爱的地界和时辰，来盗 Blackbeard 的财宝，这似乎是桩不知羞耻的事；走过旋转栅门时，我简直半等着一条毛茸茸、目光凶恶的高大身影，从教堂北面的暗影里蹿将出来。可是什么也没有动静；我踏着结霜的草，脚下嚓嚓作响，一路穿过墓园，跨过一座座坟茔，总拣没影子的地方走，朝远处那丛黑黢黢的紫杉而去。

===Original===
When I got round the yews, there was the tomb standing out white against them, and at the foot of the tomb was the hole like a patch of black velvet spread upon the ground, it was so dark. Then, for a moment, I thought that Blackbeard might be lying in wait in the bottom of the hole, and I stood uncertain whether to go on or back. I could catch the rustle of the water on the beach﻿—not of any waves, for the bay was smooth as glass, but just a lipper at the fringe; and wishing to put off with any excuse the descent into the passage, though I had quite resolved to make it, I settled with myself that I would count the water wash twenty times, and at the twentieth would let myself down into the hole. Only seven wavelets had come in when I forgot to count, for there, right in the middle of the moon’s path across the water, lay a lugger moored broadside to the beach. She was about half a mile out, but there was no mistake, for though her sails were lowered her masts and hull stood out black against the moonlight. Here was a fresh reason for delay, for surely one must consider what this craft could be, and what had brought her here. She was too small for a privateer, too large for a fishing-smack, and could not be a revenue boat by her low freeboard in the waist; and ’twas a strange thing for a boat to cast anchor in the midst of Moonfleet Bay even on a night so fine as this. Then while I watched I saw a blue flare in the bows, only for a moment, as if a man had lit a squib and flung it overboard, but I knew from it she was a contrabandier, and signalling either to the shore or to a mate in the offing. With that, courage came back, and I resolved to make this flare my signal for getting down into the hole, screwing my heart up with the thought that if Blackbeard was really waiting for me there, ’twould be little good to turn tail now, for he would be after me and could certainly run much faster than I. Then I took one last look round, and down into the hole forthwith, the same way as I had got down earlier in the day. So on that February night John Trenchard found himself standing in the heap of loose fallen mould at the bottom of the hole, with a mixture of courage and cowardice in his heart, but overruling all a great desire to get at Blackbeard’s diamond.
===Chinese===
绕过紫杉，那座坟白晃晃地衬在树影前；坟脚下便是那个洞，黑得像铺在地上的一块黑天鹅绒。一时之间，我想 Blackbeard 说不定正守在洞底，便立在那里，进退不定。我听得见海滩上水的窸窣——不是什么浪，因为湾里平得像一面玻璃，只在边上有一线细浪；我虽已决意下去，却又想随便寻个由头，把下洞这件事往后拖一拖，便同自己讲定：数那水漫上来二十回，数到第二十回，就让自己下洞去。可是只上来了七个小浪，我就忘了数——因为就在月亮横铺水面的那条光路正当中，泊着一条斜桁帆船，船舷侧对着海滩。它离岸约莫半英里，却错不了：帆虽落了，桅杆和船身衬着月光，黑黢黢地立在那里。这又添了一层耽搁的新由头，因为这条船究竟是什么来头、为何到此，总得思量思量。它当私掠船嫌小，当渔船又嫌大，船腰干舷又那样低，不可能是缉私船；况且哪怕夜色这样好，一条船跑到 Moonfleet 湾当中下锚，也是桩稀罕事。我正望着，只见船头爆出一朵蓝色的火焰，只亮了一瞬，仿佛有人点燃一枚花爆竹，随手扔下了水；可我由此明白了：这是一条走私船，正打着信号，不是给岸上，便是给外海接应的伙伴。这一来勇气回来了，我拿定主意，把这道火光当作我下洞的号令，又硬起心肠想道：Blackbeard 若果真在底下等我，这会儿掉头也没什么用处了，反正他要追我，而且必定跑得比我快得多。于是我最后环顾了一遭，随即立刻下洞，走的还是白天下来的那条路。就这样，在二月里的这个夜晚，John Trenchard 发觉自己站在洞底一堆松散的塌土上，心里一半是胆量、一半是怯懦，可是压倒一切的，是弄到 Blackbeard 那颗钻石的强烈欲望。

===Original===
Out came tinderbox and candle, and I was glad indeed when the light burned up bright enough to show that no one, at any rate, was standing by my side. But then there was the passage, and who could say what might be lurking there? Yet I did not falter, but set out on this adventurous journey, walking very slowly indeed﻿—but that was from fear of pitfalls﻿—and nerving myself with the thought of the great diamond which surely would be found at the end of the passage.
